I.E Certainty: illusory promise on the reading carried from D; Gibbs J’s dictum with its hedges and the
‘Perhaps’ exception answered with its reason (3.0; C; floor 76) [Meehan 580 to 582; Godecke 646 to 647
(Gibbs J); Pace 150 to 151 by footnote]

    Issue. Whether a promise to lend ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ is too uncertain to bind because it leaves the
    lender a discretion whether to perform, or leaves an essential matter to future agreement.
    Rules at source.
         ‘A particular promise may be illusory because the contract effectively gives the promisor an unfettered
         discretion as to whether to perform the promise.’ [CB3B 159 | [6.10] | editors]; S3B slide 3 (the categories
         ‘often overlap’; ‘courts will strive to give effect to a contract if possible’).
         ‘A consideration is illusory if its payment or fulfilment depends upon an unfettered discretion vested in the
         promisor.’ and ‘even when no objective criterion of measurement is available, it may still be possible to infer a
         promise to act honestly and/or reasonably … the consideration will not be illusory if the discretion must be
         exercised within specified parameters.’ [CB3B 166 | 150 to 151 | McHugh JA, Biotechnology Australia Pty Ltd v
         Pace | majority in result with Kirby P; Hope JA dissenting].
         Kitto J in Placer, quoted by Gibbs CJ [CB3B 183 | 581 | Gibbs CJ quoting Placer Development Ltd v
         Commonwealth at 356 | cite as quoted, never as if read].
         Gibbs CJ’s limit: ‘that principle does not apply where the discretion or option of the contracting party relates,
         not to the performance of the contractual obligations themselves, but only to the fulfilment of a condition upon
      which the contract depends’ and ‘There is a concluded agreement as to the terms of the contract which, if the
      condition is satisfied, leaves no discretion in either party as to whether he shall carry them out.’ [CB3B 183 to
      184 | 581 to 582 | Gibbs CJ, Meehan v Jones | his reasoning; unanimous result; Aickin J died before judgment]; a
      clause ‘which speaks of “satisfactory terms and conditions”’ requires the purchaser’s satisfaction; ‘No doubt it
      may be implied that the purchaser will act honestly in deciding whether or not he is satisfied.’ [CB3B 183 | 581
      (the sentence sits wholly after the [581] marker) | Gibbs CJ]; Mason J: ‘subject to an implied obligation that he
      will act honestly, or honestly and reasonably, in endeavouring to obtain finance and in deciding whether to
      accept or reject proposals for finance’ [CB3B 185 | 588 | Mason J], and ‘The limitation that the purchaser must
      act honestly, or honestly and reasonably, takes the case out of the principle that’ a promise accompanied by a
      discretion whether to perform is no contract [CB3B 185 | 589 to 590 (the sentence sits before the [590] marker,
      which falls inside the quoted principle; OFF Meehan paragraph 23 closes ‘(at p590)’) | Mason J]; ‘it must be
      reached honestly, or honestly and reasonably’ [CB3B 186 | 590 | Mason J]; Wilson J: ‘the court will imply no
      greater obligation on the purchaser than that he is obliged to act honestly in determining whether the available
      finance is satisfactory’, ‘I am inclined to think there is force in this view’, and ‘it is sufficient for me, like Mason
      J, to refrain from expressing a concluded view’ [CB3B 188 | 597 to 598 | Wilson J, who agreed with Mason J
      ‘that each of the issues in the appeal should be resolved’ alike (597) but inclined to honesty alone]; no majority
      on honest against reasonable (v10 printed ‘Mason J (Wilson J agreeing)’ against the ‘honestly, or honestly and
      reasonably’ words, which Wilson J did not adopt, and printed 580 to 581 for Gibbs CJ’s sentence; both corrected
      in v11 from the source page). The meeting of the honesty fetter, same judge and same page: ‘This point would
      have force if the contract left the court at large to assess what is “finance on satisfactory terms and conditions”,
      yielding no indication as to what the parties meant by that expression.’ [CB3B 185 | 587 (before the [588]
      marker; UPGRADES_r1 U1 printed 588) | Mason J | inner quotation never reproduced (D9): quote only ‘left the
      court at large’ or paraphrase]. The official text of 578 reads ‘subject to finance, or to suitable finance, or to
      satisfactory finance’; the casebook drops the second and third limbs (R-K).
      The course passage squarely on terms reserved to one contracting party, now CORE (CRIT_round3 M11,
      adopted as a swap funded by P and I.F2): Gibbs J, obiter: ‘it does not necessarily follow from what I have said
      that an agreement which left further terms to be settled by one of the parties, rather than by his solicitors,
      would be treated as a concluded contract’; ‘it seems to me that there would be no binding contract in such a
      case, which would fall within the principle’ in Thorby v Goldberg (quoted); ‘Perhaps it may be different where
      agreement has been reached on all essential terms but the determination of subsidiary matters has been left to
      one of the parties’; ‘it is unnecessary to express any concluded opinion on these matters’ [CB3B 190 | 646 to
      647 | Gibbs J, Godecke v Kirwan | obiter, hedged both ways; never spliced (D2)]. Both hedges travel with it:
      ‘obiter’ (or the hedge ‘doubted’) and ‘Perhaps’ for subsidiary matters; the loan’s unspecified ‘conditions’ may be
      subsidiary (amount, date, term and rate being fixed) or essential, which is why the dictum tips but does not
      decide.
      Rocky’s counter, course-extracted (credit): Walsh J (Mason J agreeing) in Godecke, construing cl 6: what the
      solicitors could require ‘must be reasonable in an objective sense, and in case of [643] dispute this is a matter
      which the court can decide’; the purchaser ‘is agreeing presently to accept as part of the bargain such
      additional provisions, if any, as are required’ [CB3B 188 to 189 | 642 to 643 | Walsh J, Mason J agreeing at 648 |
      majority reasoning]. Official-only, credit (R-L(5)): Walsh J’s adoption of Bray CJ, ‘there is no reason in principle
      for holding that there cannot be any binding contract if some matter is left to be determined by one of the
      contracting parties’, ‘subject to the qualifications to which he refers’ (not reproduced in any file) [OFF Godecke
      | paragraph 16, ‘(at p642)’ | official-only (O-11)]. Gibbs J also disagrees with Bray CJ’s observation and finds
      Sweet & Maxwell rested on ‘the familiar and objective standard of reasonableness’ [CB3B 190 | 647 | Gibbs J]
      (credit).
      Kirby P (3) ‘the court will not accept for itself a discretion which the parties have, by their agreement, reserved
      to one or other of them’ and (4) ‘Usually, there is no objectively right decision in these cases’ [CB3B 163 to 164
      | 135 | Kirby P, Pace]; McHugh JA’s Thorby line at 154 to 155 (points toward Rocky; never cited as holding
      Meehan inapplicable); Murphy J at 596 to 597 (alone); Whitlock 461 and Hall v Busst 222 (analogies only);
      MacRobertson Miller 132 to 133 (Barwick CJ; the slide’s ‘we promise to fly you unless we choose not to’ is the
      slide’s own words, S1A slide 18).
Facts (deciding). ‘subject to satisfactory conditions’ (Rocky both promisor and judge of ‘satisfactory’). Supporting
(credit): no ‘reasonably’; no object named; bullet 2’s ‘subject to success of Hail Mary Project’.
Core. (1) The issue framed on the illusory-promise criterion (the promisor’s ‘unfettered discretion’; Pace 150 to 151
or Placer via Meehan 581 in the footnote). (2) Meehan, the opponent’s best authority, distinguished on Gibbs CJ’s
own limit, not on its result: ‘satisfactory terms and conditions’ bound there because the discretion related only to
‘the fulfilment of a condition upon which the contract depends’ (finance from a third party) and there was ‘a
concluded agreement as to the terms’, whereas here, on the reading decided at D and CARRIED in terms (‘(D)’ or ‘on
the reading at D’; the counted form: ‘whereas here (D) the conditions are the loan’s own terms’), the conditions are
terms of Rocky’s own loan (or, on the external reading, the email is Meehan-shaped and not illusory); Meehan’s
similarity is NAMED before it is distinguished (its clause, like the email, turned on what was ‘satisfactory’ to the
party who would perform: UPGRADES_r1 U1, special care 1). (2A) ROCKY’S BEST ANSWER PUT AND MET in a
clause (UPGRADES_r1 U1, adopted as core; R-X(5)): an implied duty to act honestly in judging what is ‘satisfactory’
would fetter the discretion and so save the promise, as Gibbs CJ, Mason J and Wilson J in Meehan each implied at
least honesty (Murphy J, alone, apart: implying ‘honest’ ‘adds nothing’, CB3B 187, 597; UPGRADES_r1’s ‘all three …
judges in the extract’ omits him) (Gibbs CJ 581, ‘No doubt it may be implied that the purchaser will act honestly’;
Mason J 588 to 590, for whom honesty ‘or honestly and reasonably’ is what ‘takes the case out of the principle’;
Wilson J 597) and as McHugh JA allowed at Pace 151 (‘even when no objective criterion of measurement is available,
it may still be possible to infer a promise to act honestly and/or reasonably’); met on the reading decided at D: an
honest judgment fetters a choice WITHIN agreed terms and cannot supply terms never agreed, which is what
‘satisfactory conditions’ leaves to Rocky (Mason J’s own limit at 587, the point ‘would have force if the contract left
the court at large … yielding no indication as to what the parties meant’; Gibbs J’s doubt at Godecke 646 about ‘an
agreement which left further terms to be settled by one of the parties, rather than by his solicitors’). The v11 form,
after ‘whereas here (D) the conditions are the loan’s own terms’: ‘, which Rocky’s implied duty to judge ’satisfactory’
honestly cannot supply’ (+10), footnoted Meehan 581 (Gibbs CJ) or 589 to 590 (Mason J), either accepted. Honesty
only is core: ‘or honestly and reasonably’ is credit and is never stated as settled by a majority (the credit-wrong line
below stands). On the external reading at D the fetter is accepted and the promise is not illusory, and that branch is
equally Excellent. Asserting the reading at E as fact, with no carry from D, where D reached it only as ‘the better
view … likely’, is overstatement (VG: CRIT_round4 M7, adopted). (3) Gibbs J’s dictum applied with both hedges in
the same sentence AND the ‘Perhaps’ exception answered: he doubted, obiter, that terms left to one party bind,
‘Perhaps’ excepting ‘subsidiary matters’, which the unspecified ‘conditions’ arguably exceed WITH THE REASON
STATED (Gibbs J’s own contrast at 647 is a ‘vital matter (such as price or rental)’ left to one party against ‘subsidiary
matters’; the reason is that the matters left to Rocky are conditions of the loan itself, its own terms, not a subsidiary
means of carrying a settled bargain into effect, so they are arguably vital rather than subsidiary; the counted form is
‘which the loan’s own conditions arguably exceed’, the words ‘the loan’s own conditions’ carrying the reason, and
‘which conditions of the loan itself arguably exceed’ (+1) is the same reason in the critic’s words: CRIT_round5 M4,
adopted under R-S(2)); so the promise is arguably illusory (or, on the external reading at D, not). A sentence that
states the ‘Perhaps’ and then concludes ‘so … illusory’ without saying why the conditions are not subsidiary is a non
sequitur, the decisive reason missing (VG: CRIT_round4 M7, adopted); v5’s ‘which these arguably exceed’ was that
shape, marking the inference without its reason (CRIT_round5 M4). (4) A conclusion consistent with D, HEDGED TO
THE STRENGTH THE SOURCE SUPPORTS, ‘arguably’, with I.D deciding the outcome (R-X(5): certainty alone is
weak to moderate, O-11, and the honesty fetter is a real answer on the materials; v10’s ‘likely’ is from v11 one tier
too strong for this unit, an overstatement graded VG under §2), the reason in the same sentence, and the outcome
for Rocky left to D (on either reading the 12 February result stands on I.F1). The case is named in fn 12 (Godecke
646–7 (Gibbs J)); naming it in the text as well (‘In Godecke, Gibbs J, obiter, doubted …’, +2) is credit, written where
words allow, because the counted S3 follows a Meehan sentence footnoted to Gibbs CJ and a marker reading the text
alone could take ‘Gibbs J’ for the Meehan judge and then read ‘obiter’ as wrong for a judgment just applied as ratio
(CRIT_round7 m2; not fundable at slack 1; Part 9 item 39).
Credit. Walsh J (Mason J agreeing) at 642 to 643 as the counter for Rocky, met (the clause there imported an
objective reasonableness standard and left the matter to solicitors, whereas ‘satisfactory’ names no standard and
leaves the terms to the promisor himself) or accepted (with Mason J’s ‘honestly, or honestly and reasonably’ as the
fetter); Walsh J at 641 to 642 (official-only); whether the loan’s ‘conditions’ are subsidiary or essential argued in
terms; Kirby P (3) and (4); McHugh JA’s Thorby line; Gibbs CJ at 579 (the objective reading he did not prefer);
Murphy J and waiver (met: waiver protects a party who is not the promisor of the performance in issue, and gives
Rocky nothing, he having lent nothing); Hall v Busst and Milne [6.20]; Pace’s two qualifications; MacRobertson
Miller by analogy; ‘5% interest’ without ‘per annum’ and ‘1 year’ without a start answered by Upper Hunter 436 to
437; ‘the better view’ form with a ‘though’ concession.
Strength and tilt. Weak to moderate on certainty alone (O-11), decided at D; presented as contested, the verdict
‘arguably’ (R-X(5)). Either conclusion can be Excellent if it identifies that the point turns on the meaning of
‘conditions’, takes the reading decided at D, applies Gibbs CJ’s limit (not merely Meehan’s result) and deals with the
honesty fetter: met on the internal reading (it cannot supply terms never agreed), accepted on the external reading
(the promise then not illusory). The Pace result and Hope JA’s dissent (CB3B 168 to 169) are credit.
Consistency. E and D decided once each and consistently; either way the 12 February result for Rocky (no claim)
stands on I.F1.
Citation. Meehan v Jones (1982) 149 CLR 571, 580–2 (Gibbs CJ); for the honesty fetter 581 (Gibbs CJ) or 589–90
(Mason J), on its own marker, one source per marker (R-R(3)); 587 (Mason J) if the meeting is footnoted; 590 (Mason
J); Biotechnology Australia Pty Ltd v Pace (1988) 15 NSWLR 130, 150–1 (McHugh JA) or 135 (Kirby P); Godecke v

Mason J agreeing) if used, 641–2 as an official-only footnote.
Drops. VG: the implied duty to act honestly not put, or put and not met (a promise held illusory with Rocky’s best
answer unanswered: U1; R-X(5)); the verdict stated ‘likely’ or unhedged rather than ‘arguably’ (R-X(5); §2);
Meehan’s similarity not named before it is distinguished; Meehan distinguished on its result only, not on Gibbs CJ’s
limit; Gibbs J’s dictum absent, or stated without ‘Perhaps’ (D3 and Poor if stated as a holding), or the ‘Perhaps’
exception stated and not answered; the reading asserted at E without the carry from D; the issue framed without the
criterion; a conclusion inconsistent with D. Good: ‘the clause is illusory’ or ‘Meehan applies’ stated. Fair: certainty
heads listed with no application; Meehan not mentioned. Poor: McHugh JA at 154 to 155 cited as holding Meehan
inapplicable (a misreading: S-5); the Gibbs J splice (D2); Murphy J’s ‘unlimited’ discretion stated as the Court’s
holding; Placer quoted as if from its own extract; the slide’s ‘unless we choose not to’ attributed to Barwick CJ.
Omitted. Credit wrong: honest/reasonable treated as settled by a majority (VG); Walsh J 641 to 642 quoted as the
casebook’s text.
Marker comments. ‘Meehan is the closest authority and it cuts for Rocky: distinguish it on Gibbs CJ’s own limit.’
‘Rocky’s best answer is the implied duty to act honestly: put it and meet it.’ ‘This is arguable at most; certainty turns

I.F1 Privity: Rocky lent nothing; the doctrine of agency (2.5; L; floor 30) [the doctrine of agency, one source
per marker (R-R(3)): a case first under R-Z(3), Port Jackson 240 (Barwick CJ) for the criterion stated in Lord
Reid’s form; the casebook [11.70] only as a last resort, per R-A(4) as amended by R-X(1); either suffices for
the criterion]

    Issue. Even if the 12 February email bound, whether Rocky has anything to recover under it: whether the 27
    February advance was Rocky’s performance through an agent (Rocky and Grace are the email’s only parties, so
    privity of the email is not the question).
    Rules at source. ‘A party not directly involved in acts of contract formation is nevertheless a party to the contract
    when: 1. One of the parties acts as agent for the noninvolved party’ and ‘Privity rule does not apply if a person
    promised a benefit under the contract can show that one of the parties was acting as their agent’ [S4A slide 16 (PDF
    page 16; the deck prints no numbers) | slide only]; the course’s statement of what must be shown: the principal
    ‘expressly or impliedly consented to the agent acting on the principal’s behalf so as to effect the principal’s relations
    with third parties’ and, ‘with respect to the particular transaction under consideration, the agent was purporting to
    act on behalf of the principal and not solely on the agent’s own behalf’ [CB4 336 | [11.70] | editors’ note; the note
    cites no case for these two elements, only Port Jackson for ratification]. Citation, from v12 under R-Z(2) and (3), an
    ORDER OF PREFERENCE and never a cumulative stack (R-R(3); CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 2; CRIT_round4 M10,
    adopted): (1) a case first: Port Jackson 240 (Barwick CJ) states Lord Reid’s requisites as the Privy Council adopted
    them, (2) ‘the carrier by the bill contracted for the stevedore’s protection as well as for his own’ and (3) ‘the
    authority of the carrier to act for the stevedore in this respect whether antecedently or by ratification was made out’
    (OFF Port Jackson [18] ‘(at p240)’, read verbatim for v12), which are the purporting and consent elements in a case’s
    words, so a sentence stating the criterion in that form (‘AMPL was Rocky’s agent only if it contracted for him as well
    as for itself, with his authority’) is footnoted to Port Jackson 240, which does not count toward R-H’s cap of three
    because the proposition is course-stated at [11.70] (CRIT_fidelity_2 m2’s ‘analogues only’ is superseded by R-Z(3));
    Trident (Brennan J: ‘If a putative promisee is merely an agent for a third party, the third party is the promisee and is
    privy to the contract’; or Deane J: ‘If the promisee contracts as agent for a disclosed or undisclosed principal, the
    principal will ordinarily himself be, for the purposes of the requirement of privity, a party to the contract’; both
    verified in the course reading, by report, judge named, no pin: R-Y(1)) states the agency route but neither element,
    so it is cited only for the bare route; The New York Star states only the authority point (the carrier’s ‘authority to act
    on the stevedore’s behalf’, OFF New York Star) and is never the citation for purporting; (2) the case the editors’ note
    cites, Port Jackson, is (1) already; (3) withdrawn: no slide is ever cited (R-Z(2)), S4A slide 16 remaining the marker’s
    locator only; (4) last resort (R-Z(3)): Andrew Robertson and Jeannie Paterson, Contract: Cases and Materials
    (Lawbook Co, 15th ed, 2025) [11.70], at most twice in the answer, accepted without deduction where the sentence
    states the elements in the editors’ own form (‘purporting to act on behalf of the principal and not solely on the
    agent’s own behalf’), which no case states in those words; the v11 counted citation (fn 13) used it, the later
    reference being ‘Robertson and Paterson (n 13) [11.70]’ (R-A(4) as amended by R-X(1): the author order is the
    course slide’s own full citation, S1A PDF page 7, printed slide 8, ‘Andrew Robertson and Jeannie Paterson, Contract:
    Cases and Materials (Lawbook Co, 15th ed, 2025) (known as the Contracts Casebook)’, which the publisher’s listing
    matches; reading guide 1A’s ‘Paterson and Robertson’ is an informal definition by surname and yields to the full
    citation; the title page is not in the folder, so the order is a disclosed judgment call: Part 9 item 11; UPGRADES_r1
    U14). The course’s words are ‘the doctrine of agency’ ([11.10]) and ‘non-application of the privity rule: agency’
    ([11.70]); ‘the agency route’ is a sheet label, never the answer’s (R7). Novation and assignment: S4A slide 16 words
    only; Coulls at 485 to 486 holds only that the paragraph there could not operate as an equitable assignment ‘having
    regard both to its terms and to the fact that … it was not supported by consideration’.
    Facts (deciding). ‘$1,150,000’; ‘$1 million’; ‘Rocky asked’ (Rocky’s best agency fact, met: UPGRADES_r1 U5); the
    guarantee clause (AMPL took Rocky’s guarantee, the fact that answers purporting: moved back from credit to core
    in v11 under U5). Supporting (credit): ‘by 15 February’; ‘4%’ against ‘5%’; ‘Pierre agreed to provide the money’; ‘the
    $1,150,000 was advanced by AMPL’; interest ‘to AMPL’.
    Core. One sentence in the L ORDER, the hypothesis, the criterion and the deciding facts preceding the result (the
    v11 form: ‘Even if the email bound, AMPL was Rocky’s agent only if purporting to act for him, not solely for itself;
    though ’Rocky asked’, it advanced ‘$1,150,000’, not ‘$1 million’, and took his guarantee, so Rocky lent nothing’, +7
    against v10’s form: UPGRADES_r1 U5): the hypothesis (‘even if the email bound’), the operative criterion (AMPL was
    Rocky’s agent only if it purported to act for him and not solely for itself: ‘with respect to the particular transaction
    under consideration, the agent was purporting to act on behalf of the principal and not solely on the agent’s own
    behalf’, CB4 336, [11.70]; in a case’s words, Lord Reid’s requisite (2), the agent ‘contracted for the [principal’s]
    protection as well as for his own’, Port Jackson 240 (Barwick CJ), the form R-Z(3) prefers; either footnote earns the
    criterion), ROCKY’S BEST FACT MET ON THAT CRITERION (‘Rocky asked’ AMPL ‘to advance $1,150,000 to Grace’
    is conceded as going to consent; it says nothing of whom AMPL purported to act for, whereas a lender that takes its
    supposed principal’s guarantee in its own document, ‘a clause that Rocky personally guaranteed repayment of the
    Loan’, acts for itself; a different sum shows only that the email was not performed and says nothing of purporting, so
    it cannot carry the point alone: §1(b), the fact must satisfy the element), the deciding facts (‘$1,150,000’, not ‘$1
    million’; the guarantee taken), and the result that Rocky lent nothing and has nothing to recover. Under R-X(3)(c)
    I.F1 is a unit whose result is not in doubt and may be compressed, but never below these items. v4’s form, the
    verdict ‘Rocky lent nothing’ before a colon and its proof after it, is withdrawn as conclusion-first (R-R(2);
    CRIT_round4 M8, adopted; VOICE.md section 4 item 10). v10 carried the sum mismatch as the one deciding fact and
    the guarantee as credit (CRIT_round4 m5, a disclosed choice); from v11 the guarantee is core, because the sum
    mismatch alone does not go to purporting and leaves ‘Rocky asked’ unanswered (U5; Part 9 item 45, now funded
    into core); that AMPL also took Grace’s promises in its own name remains credit.
    Credit. That AMPL took Grace’s promises in its own name; the further differences (27 February, not ‘by 15
    February’; ‘4%’, not ‘5%’; the loan documented as AMPL’s own); further information, with its direction (why
    ‘$1,150,000’; whether the advance was arranged as Rocky’s loan funded by AMPL, a yes reopening agency);
    novation named only, its requirements never stated (no case in the readings states them; never cited to a slide: R-

    Strength and tilt. Strong. A conclusion that Rocky recovers under the 12 February email is Poor with the D6
    deduction.
    Drops. VG: the figures not used, or the criterion not stated, or ‘Rocky asked’ not met on the purporting criterion, or
    the result rested on the sum mismatch alone with no fact going to purporting (U5). Good: ‘AMPL lent, not Rocky’
    bare; ‘Rocky lent nothing’ stated before its proof (D5 also). Fair: the 12 February claim said to fail for want of privity
    (Rocky is a party to the email); novation asserted with requirements stated. Poor: Rocky held entitled to repayment
    under the email; assignment presented as available on non-course requirements. Omitted: the claim walked through
    D and E with no conclusion on whether Rocky has anything to recover.
    Marker comment. ‘Answer the client’s question: even if the email bound, what did Rocky lend?’

I.F2 Privity: the general rule, benefit aspect and both limbs; the Loan Agreement’s parties; the joint-promisee
argument; ‘I never signed anything with you’; the cross-reference to II.G (7.5; C; floor 81) [Coulls 486 to 487;
for the rule Coulls 494 to 495 (Windeyer J) alone, never a slide (R-Z(2); S4A slide 2 also states the party limb
only: R-U(1)); 478 credit]

    Issue. Whether Rocky is a party to Grace’s promise to repay in the Loan Agreement and gave consideration for it;
    whether his best argument (a joint promisee with AMPL) succeeds; and whether Grace’s reason (‘I never signed
    anything with you’) is the law.
    Rules at source. ‘Rule: only a person who is a party to a contract can enforce the contract and incur obligations
    under it … A contract cannot confer an enforceable benefit on a nonparty’ [S4A slide 2 | slide only; read at PDF page
    2, text layer and page image: it states the party limb (‘only a person who is a party to a contract can enforce the
    contract’) and the two aspects, and nothing about consideration, so it is never the citation for the general rule, which
    Coulls 494 to 495 states (R-P; R-U(1); CRIT_fidelity_9 Finding 1)]; the editors’ two limbs, ‘(a) are a party to (ie, privy
    to) the contract, and (b) have provided consideration’, and ‘The privity rule is separate from the rule that
    consideration must move from the promisee.’ [CB4 327 | [11.10] | editors’ note]; ‘a person not a party to a contract
    may not himself sue upon it so as directly to enforce its obligations’ [CB4 329 | 478 | Barwick CJ, Coulls v Bagot’s
    Executor & Trustee Co Ltd | dissenting on construction; a statement of accepted law, cited at [14] by Mason CJ and
    Wilson J in Trident as part of what the Court ‘has hitherto accepted’ (OFF Trident; they then departed from the rule
    for liability insurance at [32]); 478 carries the PRIVITY limb only: in the same passage Barwick CJ says ‘That C
    provided no part of the consideration seems to me irrelevant. Questions of consideration and of privity are not
    always kept distinct’, so 478 must never be pinned for the consideration limb (CRIT_fidelity_3 M4, adopted)]; ‘only
    those who are parties to a contract can sue upon it. For us that statement is incontrovertible’ [CB4 333 | 494 |
    Windeyer J | dissenting on construction]; ‘For us the rule prevails that a plaintiff who sues on a promise must show a
    consideration for it provided by him’ [CB4 333 | 495 (OFF Coulls paragraph 18 ‘(at p495)’; the casebook prints no
    [495] marker, so ‘494–9’ is accepted as the casebook-only form) | Windeyer J]; ‘The question to be determined, then,
    is whether the testator’s wife was a party to the contract and whether, upon its true construction, the contract
    contains a promise by the company to pay to her’; ‘the contract purports expressly to be one made between Arthur
    Leopold Coulls and O’Neil Construction Pty Ltd’ [CB4 330 | 486 | Taylor and Owen JJ | majority, ratio on who is a
    party]; ‘there was no express promise by the company to pay royalties to the wife nor is it possible to imply such a
    promise’ and ‘the mere fact that her signature appears does not make her a party; this is a question to be resolved
    upon a consideration of the written instrument itself’ [CB4 331 | 487 | Taylor and Owen JJ | majority, ratio]; the joint-
    promisee rule, conditional: ‘If, however, the correct conclusion is that the widow was a party to the contract it is, to
    our minds, clear that she is entitled to receive the royalties payable after her husband’s death notwithstanding that
    she, personally, gave no consideration for the company’s promise’ and ‘at common law an action to recover must
    have been brought by both promisees and that it would fail if brought by one alone’ [CB4 330 | 486 | Taylor and
    Owen JJ | majority; the course’s answer to RG4A’s ‘multi-party agreement where privity is satisfied but one party has
    not given consideration’ question]; McTiernan J: the words ‘including its heading … countervail against the inference
    that Mrs Coulls is a party, sought to be drawn from the fact that she signed it’ [CB4 331 | 482 to 483]; Windeyer J,
    dissenting, inferred an interest from her signature at 490 (‘I can see no reason for her signature except upon the
    basis that she had an interest in the performance of the contract’) [CB4 332 | 490 | dissenting on construction; the
    sentence sits before the [491] marker, which falls in the next sentence, so the pin is 490 and not ‘490 to 491’
    (CRIT_fidelity_6 Finding 5, adopted: a range one page too wide invites ‘the reference should not include’); the tie to
    joint-promisee status at 493 is official-only (OFF Coulls paragraph 13)] (credit); a party may be bound without
    signing: ‘Since Empirnall has taken the benefit of the work with knowledge of the terms on which it was offered, an
    objective bystander would conclude that Empirnall had accepted the offer’ [CB1B 72 | 536 | McHugh JA] (credit);
    formation of the Loan Agreement between parties present: ‘In the common case of contracts, whether oral or in
    writing inter praesentes, there is no difficulty’ [CB1B 88 | 41 | Lord Wilberforce, Brinkibon] (credit; Gibson 297 for
    construction of the document).
    Facts (deciding). ‘AMPL and Grace entered into a written loan agreement’; ‘I never signed anything with you’.
    Supporting (credit): ‘signed by Grace and AMPL’; interest ‘to AMPL’; ‘under the Loan Agreement with AMPL’ (the
    narrator’s description of what the demand required, not Rocky’s quoted words nor the letter’s; usable under R-D as
the proven content of the demand, never as Rocky’s admission: CRIT_fidelity_3 M7, adopted); ‘Best, Grace’; the
heading and parties clause are not narrated (further information: they decided Coulls at 482 to 483 and 486).
Core. (1) The general rule in the course’s words, benefit aspect, with the two requirements distinct (a party; and
consideration provided), footnoted to ONE source that carries both: Coulls 494 to 495 (Windeyer J dissenting), where
494 states the party limb (‘only those who are parties to a contract can sue upon it. For us that statement is
incontrovertible’) and 495 the consideration limb (‘a plaintiff who sues on a promise must show a consideration for it
provided by him’); no slide is ever that source (R-Z(2); slide 2 also states only the party limb: CRIT_fidelity_9 Finding
1; R-U(1)), a slide citation here being graded at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5); a footnote that adds Barwick CJ 478 for the
party limb cites two sources for one proposition and is over-citation under R8 (CRIT_round5 M1, adopted; R-S(1);
v5’s fn 14 did so and is corrected). (2) The test for who is a party: ‘the written instrument itself’, not signature (487).
(3) Coulls compared on its facts: Mrs Coulls signed, yet the contract ‘purports expressly to be one made between’
her husband and the company, whereas AMPL and Grace ‘entered into’ the Loan Agreement (the formation of the
Loan Agreement between them, offer, acceptance by execution and intention, being carried by that clause; the
answer never asserts that the Loan Agreement ‘purports expressly’ to be between AMPL and Grace as a narrated
fact: D4). (4) Rocky’s strongest argument named and met in a clause: his joint-promisee argument presupposes a
promise made to both, and nothing narrated makes Grace’s promise one to Rocky (the counted form names the
argument by its course name and meets it; its content, recovery ‘notwithstanding that she, personally, gave no
consideration’ (486), is credit, moved from core to fund I.E’s Gibbs J swap on the critic’s own ranking: CRIT_round3
m1 and Part 3.4; even joint promisees must sue together, credit). (5) Grace’s planted line answered: right in result,
wrong in reason, signature being neither necessary nor decisive; the consequence that Rocky’s 5 March demand
fails in his own name, with the one-clause cross-reference R-Q requires (the demand, made on 5 March 2025 for ‘the
principal’, was ARGUABLY premised on the 12 months having run from 27 February: ‘(II.G)’; the premise is an
inference from the demand’s timing against ‘repayable at the expiry of 12 months’, nothing being narrated about the
date the demand assumed, so it is written ‘arguably’ under R-M and §3 exactly as every other derived fact,
whichever party it helps: CRIT_fidelity_4 Finding 1, adopted; the counted form is ‘the 5 March demand, arguably
premised on 27 February (II.G), fails’).
Credit. Barwick CJ at 478 (‘a person not a party to a contract may not himself sue upon it so as directly to enforce
its obligations’; dissenting on construction; the privity limb only, never the consideration limb, the same passage
saying ‘That C provided no part of the consideration seems to me irrelevant’), usable as the citation for the party
limb ONLY where Windeyer J 494 to 495 is not cited for it (moved from the grid’s alternatives to credit: R-S(1)); the
T2 reconciliation in four words where words allow, ‘as to that clause only’ at II.I1 (Rocky’s assent, if found, makes
him party to the guarantee promise and not promisee of Grace’s promise to repay, so I.F2 and II.I2 do not contradict:
CRIT_round5 m12, credit, +4); limb (b) applied in a clause (no consideration moved from Rocky, AMPL having lent:
‘and Rocky gave Grace nothing for it’, +6, where words allow: CRIT_round4 m6); ‘whereas’ is kept in the counted
Coulls comparison because the sentence contrasts the instrument’s named parties in Coulls with the Loan
Agreement’s, and a sentence opened with the connective ‘Here,’ would use a signpost on R1’s list of those he never
uses (CRIT_round4 m6, second limb, rejected); R1’s list bans only that sentence-initial ‘Here,’, so a mid-sentence
locative ‘here’, as in the counted model’s ‘absent here’ (I.D S2), ‘whereas here (D)’ (I.E S2) and ‘weaker here’ (II.D
S2), is not an R1 lapse (his Part A uses neither form, so the locative is neither his signpost nor a banned one:
CRIT_round8 m3); McTiernan J’s heading reasoning; the difference from Mrs Coulls (Rocky is named as promisor of
the guarantee, which goes to the burden aspect, II.I1); Windeyer J at 490 as Rocky’s analogy, dissenting; Empirnall
536 as positive authority that a party may be bound without signing; ‘anything’ too wide as to 12 February (Grace
did confirm the email, though it gives Rocky no claim); the lay reading of Grace’s line; the a fortiori point (the only
clause naming Rocky burdens him) confined to the guarantee reading of ‘the clause’ (T18); ‘Best, Grace’ described
as a sign-off, never asserted to be a signature in law (the materials state no rule); the Loan Agreement’s acceptance
and intention in a clause each (a company’s written interest-bearing loan objectively intended to bind; presumption
language never the test). From v11 (UPGRADES_r1 U18, credit only): the rule stated with the joint-promisee
qualification (‘Only a party who provided consideration, joint promisees aside, can enforce a contract’, +3) is credit,
the course’s general rule being ‘(a) are a party to (ie, privy to) the contract, and (b) have provided consideration’
(CB4 327, [11.10]) and the exception being put and met by core (4); rule then exception is the IRAC order, not a
misstatement.
Strength and tilt. Strong; authority decides. Rocky held a party able to enforce the Loan Agreement is Poor with
the D6 deduction. Grace’s defence accepted because Rocky did not sign is Good (right result, wrong reason: R-R(4)),
the grade II.I1 gives the mirror error; v9’s Poor with the D6 deduction for it is withdrawn, the result being right
(Coulls 486 to 487) and D6 being confined to confidently wrong conclusions (CRIT_round9 M2; R-U(3)).
Consistency. The same rule (signature not the test) applies to Rocky’s own non-signature at II.I1 (T10); an answer
that tells Grace signature is irrelevant and then lets Rocky escape the guarantee because he ‘never signed’ is not
Excellent on either unit.
Citation. Coulls v Bagot’s Executor & Trustee Co Ltd (1967) 119 CLR 460, 486 or 487 (Taylor and Owen JJ), each
footnote pinned to its own sentence (486 for ‘purports expressly’ and the joint-promisee rule; 487 for signature and
‘no express promise’); for the general rule, 494–5 (Windeyer J dissenting) ALONE, 494 carrying the party limb and
495 the consideration limb (‘For us the rule prevails that a plaintiff who sues on a promise must show a
consideration for it provided by him’, the official text marking the page as 495), never a slide (R-Z(2); slide 2 states
the party limb only in any event: R-U(1)) (the counted fn 14 reads ‘Coulls v Bagot’s Executor & Trustee Co Ltd (1967)
119 CLR 460, 494–5 (Windeyer J dissenting)’); 478 (Barwick CJ dissenting) stacked with 494–5 for the one sentence
is over-citation (VG: CRIT_round5 M1; R-S(1)); 478 for the consideration limb is a citation fault (VG; the passage
argues the contrary); a pin of 494 alone for the consideration sentence is a citation imprecision (VG at item 10; ‘the
     reference should not include’), not a Fair (CRIT_fidelity_2 m11, adopted). The AGLC form for a dissent is ‘(Barwick
     CJ dissenting)’, no comma and no qualifier; ‘on construction’ belongs in the text if wanted (CRIT_round3 m13,
     adopted).
     Drops. VG: Coulls not compared on its facts; the joint-promisee argument not named, or named without the ‘promise
     to both’ answer; Grace’s reasoning neither corrected nor read as the privity reason; only one limb; a dissent relied
     on without its status; the II.G cross-reference missing; [11.10] cited for the rule where Coulls 494 to 495 states it (a

I.F3 Privity: circumventing the privity rule, in a line (1.0; L; floor 20) [cited to a case: eg Trident (Brennan J)
or Trident (Mason CJ and Wilson J), by report, judge named, no pin (R-Y(1), (2); R-Z(2), (3))]

     Rules at source. The five ways: ‘First, the contracting party may enforce the contract … Secondly, it might be
     possible to argue that the privity doctrine has no application … by virtue of the doctrine of agency … Thirdly, C may
     be able to establish that … B was acting as a “trustee” for C … Fourthly, C may be able to establish an estoppel
     against B … Fifthly … damages under the Australian Consumer Law’ [CB4 328 | [11.10] | editors]; S4A slide 3 (point
     1 cites Beswick v Beswick; point 3 reverses the trustee formulation; a locator only, never cited: R-Z(2)). Citation from
     v12 (R-Y(1), (2); R-Z(2), (3); R-J and R-N superseded, so there is no cap of one Trident footnote and no allocation
     between units): a Trident judge who states the routes, by report, judge named, no pin, one source per marker.
     Verified in the course reading (corpus ‘Trident (course reading)’, by judge): Brennan J names the three principles
     that might avail a non-party, ‘It was not suggested that agency, trust or estoppel availed McNiece’, and sets out trust
     and agency each as a ‘so-called exception’ (‘The first so-called exception is found in the law of trusts. A promisee
     may be or become a trustee of the promise for a third party’; ‘A second so-called exception is found in the law of
     agency’), adding that ‘Neither the principles of trust nor the principles of agency are exceptions to the doctrine of
     privity’ (they operate around it, which is why the answer calls them routes around the rule, never exceptions to it);
     Mason CJ and Wilson J name trust and agency, the third party being ‘dependent on the willingness of the promisee
     to exercise his rights, in the absence of a trust, an agency relationship or an enforceable agreement between the
     promisee and the third party’, and recount the equitable principle that ‘a party to a contract can constitute himself a
     trustee for a third party of a right under a contract so that the third party can enforce the promise’; Toohey J refers
     to ‘the various ways in which third parties have been permitted to sue, whether by way of trust, agency or the like’.
     Brennan J is the fullest single source for a line naming agency, trust and estoppel; Mason CJ and Wilson J serve for
     trust and agency; either is accepted and neither is deducted. The first route, the contracting party (AMPL) enforcing,
     needs no separate footnote, being the fact that AMPL ‘contacted Grace directly’. The casebook [11.10] is a last
     resort only (R-Z(3)) and is not needed here, Trident stating the routes; a slide is never cited (R-Z(2)). Windeyer J at
     502 (‘I am not persuaded that C could force A to seek redress from B’) [CB4 335 | 502 | dissenting on construction;
     on the hypothesis of a non-party] (credit).
     Facts (deciding). ‘AMPL contacted Grace directly’. Supporting: ‘AMPL required the $1,150,000’; no representation
     by Grace to Rocky.
     Core. One sentence in the L form, the facts preceding the result (‘AMPL enforces its own loan, agency fails and no
     trust or estoppel appears, so no route around the rule assists’; v10’s ‘AMPL enforces its own money’ is a wrong
     collocation, money not being enforced, and is from v11 an R1 lapse: UPGRADES_r1 U29, zero words; ‘AMPL, the
     contracting party, would enforce for itself’ (+3), which names the first route in the casebook’s words (‘First, the
     contracting party may enforce the contract’, CB4 328), is equally Excellent; never ‘is narrated’: R-R(1)), disposing of
     the routes: the contracting party (AMPL) is already enforcing its own loan; agency fails (F1); no trust for Rocky or
     assumption he relied on appears on the facts.
     Credit. Windeyer J at 502; assignment and novation named only, with no stated requirements (no case in the
     readings states them). The statutory route is not covered and is never named as outside Weeks 1 to 4 or any scope
     (R-Z(1), (4)); v11’s credit ‘The statutory route named as outside Weeks 1 to 4’ is withdrawn, and writing it is graded
     at R1 and R8 under R-Z(5).
     Drops. VG: one route missing. Good: ‘no exception applies’ bare, or a verdict followed by its reasons (D5 also). Fair:
     Trident’s trust stated as the route with no facts. Poor: assignment presented as available on stated non-course
     requirements; Rocky said able to sue as trustee beneficiary. Omitted.
     Marker comment. ‘Try every route for the non-party, briefly, on the facts.’
